## Supplementary Methods

## Statistics &amp; Reproducibility

We executed quality control by running the ENIGMA 1 Cerebellum Volumetric Pipeline QC Scripts with Singularity 1 . We utilized the QC pipeline of ACAPULCO 2  as it detects the number of outliers out of 28 regions and address any missegmentations or outliers. As there are thousands of brain scans, we were unable to inspect each one individually but inspected around 5% of the scans manually. The output of the quality control, 'QC\_Images.html', provides visual representations of the ACAPULCO 3  segmented images in coronal, sagittal and transverse sections. These images allow for a visual inspection of the segmentation quality and help identify any mis-segmentations or abnormalities in the cerebellar lobules. In addition to the visual assessment, the QC pipeline also provides quantitative and visual information about the cerebellum volumetrics of the segmented regions. This includes measures such as volume, outliers, and box plots of the outliers. These metrics can be used to identify extreme outliers in terms of the number of lobules, indicating potential segmentation errors or other anomalies. We initially started with 58,709 individuals without diagnoses and 3,764 diagnosed individuals, consisting of both ACAPULCO outputs and relevant covariates. No statistical method was used to predetermine sample size. We excluded participants if their scans denoted outliers in at least 2 regions and if each scanning-site had fewer than 5 participants. The individuals without diagnoses were then divided into training (TR) and testing (TE) sets, by selecting every second participant into TR and TE respectively account for sex and scanning-site to be matched. This division resulted in 27,117 individuals in TR and 26,985 individuals in TE.

## Atlases

The complex organization of the cerebellum suggests that different subregions of the cerebellum play specialized roles in various cognitive and motor functions. Stoodley and Schmahmann 3  showed functions based on lobules where lobule V/adjacent VI/lobule VIII were activated by sensorimotor tasks, lobule VIIIA/B for motor activation, VIIIB for somatosensory activation, posterior lobes for higher level tasks, VI and Crus I for language and verbal working memory, lobule VI for spatial tasks, VI/Crus I/VIIB for executive functions, and VI/Crus I/medial VII for emotional processing. However, the functional subregions of the cerebellum do not align with its lobular boundaries, as demonstrated in the MDTB map 4 , where regions 1 and 2 are associated with hand movements, 3 with visual memory, 4 with attention, regions 5 and 6 with working memory, regions 7 and 8 to narrative comprehension, regions 8 and 9 to language functions and region 10 to autobiographical recall. These functional mappings illustrate that the boundaries of cerebellar functions do not strictly adhere to the anatomical lobular divisions. Instead, functional boundaries cross lobules, indicating a more nuanced organization of cerebellar functions that may cut across traditional anatomical landmarks. The work by Buckner et al.  5,6  depicted the complex interconnectivity between networks exhibits common functional activations even in the absence of a specific task. By assigning cerebellar voxels to distinct cerebral networks based on their

the cerebellum and the and the cerebral cortex during resting state. The resting state highest temporal correlation, the study revealed the rich functional connections of the cerebellum with various regions of the cortex. This indicates that the cerebellum is

involved in ongoing functional interactions with the cerebral cortex that contribute to overall brain functioning. The global topographic organization of the cerebellum, as revealed by the study, shows specific patterns of connectivity between different cerebellar lobules and cortical regions. For instance, lobule VIII and the anterior lobe exhibit connectivity with cortical and premotor areas belonging to networks 3, 4, and 7. Lobule VII and IX, on the other hand, demonstrate connectivity with prefrontal and parietal association areas within networks 8, 12, 13, 14, 16, and 17. The regions for the 17 networks are N1: Visual A, N2: Visual B; Network 3: Somatomotor A; N4: Somatomotor B; N5: Dorsal Attention A; N6: Dorsal Attention B; N7: Salience/ Ventral Attention A; N8: Salience/ Ventral Attention B; N9: Limbic B; N10: Limbic A; N11: Control A; N12: Control B; N13: Control C; N14: Default A; N15: Default B; N16: Default C; N17: Temporal Parietal. Understanding the resting state functional connectivity of the cerebellum provides valuable insights into its contributions to overall brain function and its involvement in both task-based and intrinsic functional networks.

## Cognition and Symptom Scores

The ASD scores examined in the correlation analyses included measures (communication, reciprocal social interaction, and stereotyped behaviours) from testing batteries that calculated Autism Diagnostic Observation Schedule (ADOS) 7 and full-scale IQ (FIQ), verbal IQ (VIQ) and performance IQ (PIQ) from ABIDE dataset. The intelligence tests included in the multisite ABIDE I dataset consists of FIQ tests from Differential Ability Scale II (DAS-II), Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale (WAIS), Wechsler Abbreviated Scale of Intelligence (WASI), Wechsler Intelligence Scale of Children (WISC), Hamburg-Wechsler Intelligence Test for children (HAWIK-

IV), Wortschatztest (WST) and Groninger Intelligence Test (GIT). VIQ consists from DAS-II, WASI, WISC, WAIS, Peabody Picture Vocabulary Test (PPVT) and Stanford Binet (STANFORD). PIQ consists of tests from DAS-II, WASI, WISC, WAIS, RAVENS, STANFORD. In ABIDE II dataset, FIQ consists of tests from Differential Ability Scale (DAS), Kaufman Brief Intelligence Test (KBIT-2), Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale (WAIS), Wechsler Abbreviated Scale of Intelligence (WASI), Wechsler Intelligence Scale of Children (WISC), and Wechsler Preschool and Primary Scale of Intelligence (WPPSI-III). VIQ consist of DAS, WAIS, WASI, WISC and WPPSI-III. PIQ consist of tests from DAS, Raven's Progressive Matrices (Raven), Snijders-Oomen Nonverbal Intelligence (SON-R), WAIS, WASI, WISC and WPPSI-III. Positive and Negative Syndrome Scale (PANSS) 8  examines the severity of patients with SZ which was divided into positive and negative scale from Oslo TOP dataset. There were some participants with missing cognition and symptom scores resulting in number of ASD of 236 and SZ of 269.

## Matched sensitivity analyses

For supplementary analyses, we conducted statistical comparisons between clinical cohorts and their sex-, age-, and scanning-site-matched groups. We examined the effect of ethnicity by matching it to the sex-, age-, and scanning-sitematched groups of SZ and BD. Additionally, clinical cohorts and individuals without diagnoses were matched using an R package, MatchIt 9 , with the argument of nearest neighbour matching sex, age and scanning-site (Supplementary Table 2B2H). Furthermore, we investigated the subgroups of ASD, as defined by DSM-IV, which include Asperger's Syndrome (AS) (n=138) and individuals with Autism (n=404) (Supplementary Table 2B-2H).

## Total antipsychotic medication load calculation

The total antipsychotic medication load is computed by converting the patients' daily dosages into standardized measures of antipsychotic exposures by dividing by the DDD (defined daily dose) value per drug. Since there are up to three types of drugs per patient, we calculated the sum: (AP1 daily dose / AP1 DDD) + (AP2 daily dose / AP2 DDD) + (AP3 daily dose / AP3 DDD). The association was measured using Spearman correlation and corrected for multiple comparison of regions of interest and direction.

## Supplementary Results

## Matched comparison

In addition, we conducted repeated analyses for sex-, age-, and scanningsite-matched groups to address concerns regarding the use of the full-sample, particularly regarding differences in age range and sample sizes. Results are presented in Supplementary Figures 6 and Supplementary Tables 19-21. As can be seen, these matched control analyses yielded very similar, but statistically weaker group effects relative to analyses using the full sample (Fig. 3B-C). Thus, the fullsample analyses demonstrate better sensitivity in detecting individual-level deviations due to larger statistical power. In DSM-V 10 , Asperger syndrome (AS) and other autism categories fall under ASD. Based on a categorization based on an older version of the DSM-IV 11 , we examined these subgroups. In contrast to Autism, AS do not have significant delay in language and cognitive development, differences that could potentially be related to the degree of cerebellar involvement 12 . These two subgroups of ASD, Asperger's

Uncorrected results from the subgroup analyses revealed a pattern that was largely in line with the full-sample analyses (Supplementary Fig. 8). However, only the increased negative deviations in the Autism subgroup survived multiple comparison correction, possibly due to reduced statistical power and the sample size difference, where Autism (n=404) is larger than AS (n=138).

## Ethnicity comparison

Using the local TOP sample, we repeated atlas-based case control analyses in the SZ and BD population to examine potential effects of ethnicity. Out of 312 SZ individuals, the data consisted of European (n=240), African (n=24), Asian (n=20), Arab people (n=10), Latin American (n=4), American (n=2), Mixed (n=9), Other (n=3), and one missing. BD consisted of European (n=251), African (n=6), Asian (n=8), Arab people (n=3), Latin American (n=1), Mixed (n=6), and Other (n=2) out of 277 individuals. Therefore, the additional analyses represented majority of the ethnicity, European. The subsample of BD consisted of 232 individuals (mean [SD] age, 33.96 [11.96] years; 88 [37.93%] male) with 232 matched controls (mean [SD] age, 29.28 [9.44] years; 100 [43.10%] male). For SZ, there were 240 individuals (mean [SD] age, 29.21 [9.10] years; 143 [59.58%] male) and 240 matched controls (mean [SD] age, 30.11 [10.14] years; 157 [65.41%] male). We found that European show almost identical patterns to the full-sample in the uncorrected comparison (Supplementary Fig. 7A-B and Supplementary Tables 22-24).

## Medication Effect in SZ

We examined the effects of drugs in individuals with SZ (Supplementary Tables 25-27). However, we did not find any significant associations between the percentage of extreme positive or negative deviations and total antipsychotic medication load. While some studies point to a reduction in brain volume as a consequence of antipsychotic treatment 13 , an alternative finding 14  showed structural volumetric increases in the striatum, possibly as a result of long-term blockade of dopamine D2 receptors.
